I remember that sudden feeling of, “What is that?” and everybody sort of turning their heads.\n\nBut then after that… there’s a gap. A real gap. I’ve tried to force it, and I’ve tried to go over it in my head like if I line up the pieces enough times, the middle will come back, but it doesn’t. I remember the shift, and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s this whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank. No matter how hard I try, I can’t piece it together.\n\nAnd that’s one of the worst parts, honestly, because I feel like I should know. I was there. I was right there, or close enough. People have asked me what happened, what I saw, what order things happened in, and I hate that I can’t give a proper answer. I can tell you what I remember before, and I can tell you what I remember after — the yelling, the confusion, seeing him down — but the middle is just… gone.\n\nDuring it, I don’t even know what I felt for that missing part. Afterward, I remember feeling stunned, like my body was moving but my mind hadn’t caught up. And then this horrible pressure, like I was supposed to understand what had just happened and I couldn’t. I still can’t, not all the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced flashbacks where you feel like you’re reliving the accident, like it’s happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I didn’t used to call them that, because I thought a flashback meant you completely didn’t know where you were or something, but… yes, that happens to me.\n\nIt can be something small. A cart hitting a seam in the floor, that sharp metal clank, and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or the smell of hot oil — not even strong, just that warm machinery smell — and suddenly I’m not just remembering it. I’m back in that bay. It’s like the room I’m standing in drops away for a second and I can see him there by the machine.\n\nIt feels very present. That’s the part that scares me. It’s not like thinking, “Oh, that reminds me of the accident.” It’s more like it’s in front of me again, like I’m seeing it happen all over, and I get this horrible rush through me. I can’t always tell how long it lasts. Sometimes it’s probably only a few seconds, but it doesn’t feel like that when it’s happening.\n\nAnd then after, I’m left standing there trying to act normal, trying to get my bearings, and I feel embarrassed because maybe nobody else even noticed anything happened. But inside, it feels like I’ve just been dragged right back into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting recklessly or doing things that could be dangerous, either on purpose or without thinking about the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it reckless, exactly. I mean… not in the way I think of that word. I’m not out looking for danger, if that’s what you mean. I’m not trying to hurt myself or prove something.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. There are times I do things too fast, or I’m not as careful as I used to be, but I don’t know if that’s recklessness or just being distracted or upset. I haven’t really thought of it as, “I’m doing something dangerous.” It’s more like afterward I might think, “Why did I handle that like that?” But I’m not sure that’s the same thing.\n\nSo… I don’t think so, not clearly. Nothing comes to mind where I’d say I was deliberately taking risks or doing dangerous things on purpose. If you mean something more specific, maybe ask me that way, because I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you often feel negative emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame that don’t seem to go away, even when you try to push them aside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I have feelings about it, of course, but whether they’re there all the time and don’t go away… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I think I’m angry, but then I’m not sure if it’s anger or just being worn down. And guilt or shame — I don’t know. Those words feel so big when you say them like that. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just having a bad day, or if I’m upset for a reason. Fear, maybe at times, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant or like it never leaves.\n\nI guess what I can say is I do get emotional about it, and I don’t always have as much control over that as I’d like. But I’m not sure I can honestly sort it into fear, anger, guilt, or shame and say, yes, this is always there. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a persistent sense of guilt, shame, or feeling like you are a bad person since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to what it is. Maybe not “I’m a bad person” in the simple way, like I think I’m evil or something, but I do feel… marked by it. Like something about me got ruined in a way I can’t explain.\n\nI spent so many years being good at that job. I knew what I was looking at, I knew when something sounded wrong, when something didn’t look right, how to catch things other people might miss. That was part of how I saw myself. And now some days I feel like damaged goods, honestly. Like I can’t even trust myself to do the work I built my whole life around. That makes me feel ashamed, even saying it.\n\nAnd I don’t trust that plant anymore. I don’t trust the people running it, either. I keep thinking, if things had been different, if people had cared more, if somebody had taken things seriously… I don’t know. That’s where my mind goes. So then it gets all tangled up — anger at them, shame at myself, guilt that I’m not handling it better.\n\nSo yes, there is this kind of persistent feeling that I’m not the same person, and not in a good way. Like I’m broken now, or less capable, and I hate admitting that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even when I know nothing is happening, I’m still bracing for something.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body just refuses to power down. I’ll lie there and lie there, and the room is quiet but I’m not quiet inside. Sometimes it gets so late it’s almost morning before I actually fall asleep, like near dawn, and then I know I have to get up again soon, which just makes it worse.\n\nAnd when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I’ll jerk awake from dreams, and in the dreams there are line alarms going off, and that awful metal sound… I can hear it so clearly that when I wake up I’m still kind of listening for it. My heart’s going and I have to figure out where I am, like I’m not fully back yet.\n\nSo even if I manage a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I get up feeling like I never really slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having intrusive thoughts or memories about a specific event that’s been bothering you, especially ones that come up even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s not just remembering it in a normal way, like thinking about something that happened. It comes in when I’m not trying to think about it at all.\n\nSometimes it’s something small that sets it off. A cart will clank at work, just that sharp metal sound, and all of a sudden I’m not really standing where I am anymore. I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil… that one is bad. It’s like the smell gets in my throat, and then I can see my coworker by the machine again, like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nI don’t want to see it. I really don’t. I’ll be doing something ordinary, trying to keep my hands busy, trying to stay focused, and then there it is. The machine, the bay, that moment. It feels so clear, not fuzzy like an old memory should feel. It just pushes its way in, and I can’t always make it stop once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling emotionally numb or detached from the people around you, like you’re not really connecting with them even when you want to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I know I’ve been different with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it numb. Sometimes I feel like I’m there but I’m also watching myself be there, if that makes sense. Like somebody will be talking to me and I know I’m supposed to respond in a normal way, so I nod or say the right thing, but inside I’m kind of… blank. Or not blank exactly, just far away.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as being detached. I still care about people. I don’t want them to think I don’t. It’s more like I don’t always have the energy to reach across that space, even when I can tell they’re trying. And then I feel bad, because I’m probably coming off cold or distracted, and that’s not what I mean to do.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really known what to call it. I just know I don’t feel as connected as I think I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, activities, or situations because they remind you of something upsetting or stressful?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I probably admit out loud.\n\nAt work, I go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay. I’ll take a longer route, even if it doesn’t make sense, even if someone would look at it and say I’m wasting steps. I know exactly where I’m turning and why. I’ll pretend I need to check something else first, or I’ll circle around so I don’t have to go by there. It’s not like I stand there making a big decision every time — it’s automatic now. I just… don’t want to be near it.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If someone brings it up, I try to shut it down or change the subject. I’ll say I don’t remember much, or I don’t want to get into it, or I’ll suddenly need to do something. It’s not because I don’t care. It’s because once people start asking questions, I can feel myself getting pulled back into it, and I just can’t do that, especially not standing there at work like everything is normal.\n\nEven outside of work, there are things I’ve stopped doing. I used to like going to those industrial-history talks — old factories, old machinery, how things were built, that kind of thing. I know that probably sounds dry, but I enjoyed it. But after the accident, the pictures of the old machines started bothering me. They’d put up slides, big gears, belts, presses, metal parts, and I’d feel myself tense up before I could even think. So I stopped going. I told myself I was just busy or tired, but really I didn’t want to sit in a room and get hit with those images.\n\nSo yes, I avoid things. I don’t like that I do, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re more angry or irritable even in situations where there’s no real threat or reason for it, like a constant sense of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually… that’s part of why I’m here, if I’m being honest. I keep snapping at people, and sometimes it’s like I hear myself doing it a second too late.\n\nThere was a safety meeting not long ago, and someone started talking through a procedure, and I don’t even remember them saying anything that bad. It wasn’t like anyone was attacking me. But I felt this heat come up in me, just instant, and before I could slow down I was yelling. Not raising my voice a little — yelling. In front of everybody. And once I started, I couldn’t seem to rein it back in fast enough.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act different around me. They’re careful. They’ll come up like they’re afraid to ask me a question, or they’ll ask someone else instead. I can see it on their faces, and that makes me feel awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I used to be someone people could come to. I knew the job, I’d explain things, I didn’t mind helping. Now I feel like they’re all waiting to see if I’m going to go off.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. And it scares me a little, honestly, because most of the time there isn’t any real reason for it. I just feel wound tight, like everything hits me wrong, and then my mouth is already moving before I’ve had a chance to think.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the accident you mentioned earlier? Specifically, what happened, and how did you feel during and after it?\n\nPatient: I can try. It’s hard because part of what bothers me is that I don’t have it in one clean piece, like a story I can just tell from start to finish.\n\nI remember being on shift. I remember normal things before it — the floor, the noise, people moving around, the kind of routine you don’t think about because you’ve done it so many times. And I remember the first alarm starting. That part is there. I can hear it when I think about it, or I think I can. I remember that sudden feeling of, “What is that?” and everybody sort of turning their heads.\n\nBut then after that… there’s a gap. A real gap. I’ve tried to force it, and I’ve tried to go over it in my head like if I line up the pieces enough times, the middle will come back, but it doesn’t. I remember the shift, and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s this whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank. No matter how hard I try, I can’t piece it together.\n\nAnd that’s one of the worst parts, honestly, because I feel like I should know. I was there. I was right there, or close enough. People have asked me what happened, what I saw, what order things happened in, and I hate that I can’t give a proper answer. I can tell you what I remember before, and I can tell you what I remember after — the yelling, the confusion, seeing him down — but the middle is just… gone.\n\nDuring it, I don’t even know what I felt for that missing part. Afterward, I remember feeling stunned, like my body was moving but my mind hadn’t caught up. And then this horrible pressure, like I was supposed to understand what had just happened and I couldn’t. I still can’t, not all the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced flashbacks where you feel like you’re reliving the accident, like it’s happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I didn’t used to call them that, because I thought a flashback meant you completely didn’t know where you were or something, but… yes, that happens to me.\n\nIt can be something small. A cart hitting a seam in the floor, that sharp metal clank, and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or the smell of hot oil — not even strong, just that warm machinery smell — and suddenly I’m not just remembering it. I’m back in that bay. It’s like the room I’m standing in drops away for a second and I can see him there by the machine.\n\nIt feels very present. That’s the part that scares me. It’s not like thinking, “Oh, that reminds me of the accident.” It’s more like it’s in front of me again, like I’m seeing it happen all over, and I get this horrible rush through me. I can’t always tell how long it lasts. Sometimes it’s probably only a few seconds, but it doesn’t feel like that when it’s happening.\n\nAnd then after, I’m left standing there trying to act normal, trying to get my bearings, and I feel embarrassed because maybe nobody else even noticed anything happened. But inside, it feels like I’ve just been dragged right back into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting recklessly or doing things that could be dangerous, either on purpose or without thinking about the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it reckless, exactly. I mean… not in the way I think of that word. I’m not out looking for danger, if that’s what you mean. I’m not trying to hurt myself or prove something.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. There are times I do things too fast, or I’m not as careful as I used to be, but I don’t know if that’s recklessness or just being distracted or upset. I haven’t really thought of it as, “I’m doing something dangerous.” It’s more like afterward I might think, “Why did I handle that like that?” But I’m not sure that’s the same thing.\n\nSo… I don’t think so, not clearly. Nothing comes to mind where I’d say I was deliberately taking risks or doing dangerous things on purpose. If you mean something more specific, maybe ask me that way, because I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you often feel negative emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame that don’t seem to go away, even when you try to push them aside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I have feelings about it, of course, but whether they’re there all the time and don’t go away… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I think I’m angry, but then I’m not sure if it’s anger or just being worn down. And guilt or shame — I don’t know. Those words feel so big when you say them like that. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just having a bad day, or if I’m upset for a reason. Fear, maybe at times, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant or like it never leaves.\n\nI guess what I can say is I do get emotional about it, and I don’t always have as much control over that as I’d like. But I’m not sure I can honestly sort it into fear, anger, guilt, or shame and say, yes, this is always there. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a persistent sense of guilt, shame, or feeling like you are a bad person since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to what it is. Maybe not “I’m a bad person” in the simple way, like I think I’m evil or something, but I do feel… marked by it. Like something about me got ruined in a way I can’t explain.\n\nI spent so many years being good at that job. I knew what I was looking at, I knew when something sounded wrong, when something didn’t look right, how to catch things other people might miss. That was part of how I saw myself. And now some days I feel like damaged goods, honestly. Like I can’t even trust myself to do the work I built my whole life around. That makes me feel ashamed, even saying it.\n\nAnd I don’t trust that plant anymore. I don’t trust the people running it, either. I keep thinking, if things had been different, if people had cared more, if somebody had taken things seriously… I don’t know. That’s where my mind goes. So then it gets all tangled up — anger at them, shame at myself, guilt that I’m not handling it better.\n\nSo yes, there is this kind of persistent feeling that I’m not the same person, and not in a good way. Like I’m broken now, or less capable, and I hate admitting that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d say that, no… not in a clear way. I mean, I haven’t really been thinking about hobbies in a neat category like that, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had some big list of things I loved doing and now I can point to it and say, “I don’t care about any of this anymore.”

A cart hitting a seam in the floor, that sharp metal clank, and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. It’s like the room I’m standing in drops away for a second and I can see him there by the machine.\n\nIt feels very present. That’s the part that scares me. I can’t always tell how long it lasts. But inside, it feels like I’ve just been dragged right back into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting recklessly or doing things that could be dangerous, either on purpose or without thinking about the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it reckless, exactly. I mean… not in the way I think of that word. I’m not out looking for danger, if that’s what you mean. I’m not trying to hurt myself or prove something.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. There are times I do things too fast, or I’m not as careful as I used to be, but I don’t know if that’s recklessness or just being distracted or upset. I haven’t really thought of it as, “I’m doing something dangerous.” It’s more like afterward I might think, “Why did I handle that like that?” But I’m not sure that’s the same thing.\n\nSo… I don’t think so, not clearly. Nothing comes to mind where I’d say I was deliberately taking risks or doing dangerous things on purpose. If you mean something more specific, maybe ask me that way, because I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you often feel negative emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame that don’t seem to go away, even when you try to push them aside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I have feelings about it, of course, but whether they’re there all the time and don’t go away… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I think I’m angry, but then I’m not sure if it’s anger or just being worn down. And guilt or shame — I don’t know. Those words feel so big when you say them like that. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just having a bad day, or if I’m upset for a reason. Fear, maybe at times, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant or like it never leaves.\n\nI guess what I can say is I do get emotional about it, and I don’t always have as much control over that as I’d like. But I’m not sure I can honestly sort it into fear, anger, guilt, or shame and say, yes, this is always there. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a persistent sense of guilt, shame, or feeling like you are a bad person since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to what it is. Maybe not “I’m a bad person” in the simple way, like I think I’m evil or something, but I do feel… marked by it. Like something about me got ruined in a way I can’t explain.\n\nI spent so many years being good at that job. I knew what I was looking at, I knew when something sounded wrong, when something didn’t look right, how to catch things other people might miss. That was part of how I saw myself. And now some days I feel like damaged goods, honestly. Like I can’t even trust myself to do the work I built my whole life around. That makes me feel ashamed, even saying it.\n\nAnd I don’t trust that plant anymore. I don’t trust the people running it, either. I keep thinking, if things had been different, if people had cared more, if somebody had taken things seriously… I don’t know. That’s where my mind goes. So then it gets all tangled up — anger at them, shame at myself, guilt that I’m not handling it better.\n\nSo yes, there is this kind of persistent feeling that I’m not the same person, and not in a good way. Like I’m broken now, or less capable, and I hate admitting that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no… not in a clear way. I mean, I haven’t really been thinking about hobbies in a neat category like that, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had some big list of things I loved doing and now I can point to it and say, “I don’t care about any of this anymore.”\n\nI’m 59, I’ve worked a long time, and a lot of my day-to-day has always been pretty practical — work, keeping up with things, getting through the week. So when you ask about pleasure or activities, I kind of have to stop and think, “What did I used to do just for enjoyment?” And I don’t have a clean answer ready.\n\nThere are times I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest. Sometimes it feels more like I’m tired, or my mind’s somewhere else, or I just don’t have the patience for things. But I can’t honestly say, yes, I’ve lost interest in everything I used to enjoy. I’ll be trying to focus, but if someone moves behind me or a door opens suddenly, I notice it right away. I’m checking where people are, where the exits are, who’s coming in and out. It’s not that I want to be doing that. It’s just… my body won’t believe I’m safe, I guess. Even when I know nothing is happening, I’m still bracing for something.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body just refuses to power down. I’ll lie there and lie there, and the room is quiet but I’m not quiet inside. Sometimes it gets so late it’s almost morning before I actually fall asleep, like near dawn, and then I know I have to get up again soon, which just makes it worse.\n\nAnd when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I’ll jerk awake from dreams, and in the dreams there are line alarms going off, and that awful metal sound… I can hear it so clearly that when I wake up I’m still kind of listening for it. My heart’s going and I have to figure out where I am, like I’m not fully back yet.\n\nSo even if I manage a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I get up feeling like I never really slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having intrusive thoughts or memories about a specific event that’s been bothering you, especially ones that come up even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s not just remembering it in a normal way, like thinking about something that happened. It comes in when I’m not trying to think about it at all.\n\nSometimes it’s something small that sets it off. A cart will clank at work, just that sharp metal sound, and all of a sudden I’m not really standing where I am anymore. I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil… that one is bad. It’s like the smell gets in my throat, and then I can see my coworker by the machine again, like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nI don’t want to see it. I really don’t. I’ll be doing something ordinary, trying to keep my hands busy, trying to stay focused, and then there it is. The machine, the bay, that moment. It feels so clear, not fuzzy like an old memory should feel. It just pushes its way in, and I can’t always make it stop once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling emotionally numb or detached from the people around you, like you’re not really connecting with them even when you want to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I know I’ve been different with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it numb. Sometimes I feel like I’m there but I’m also watching myself be there, if that makes sense. Like somebody will be talking to me and I know I’m supposed to respond in a normal way, so I nod or say the right thing, but inside I’m kind of… blank. Or not blank exactly, just far away.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as being detached. I still care about people. I don’t want them to think I don’t. It’s more like I don’t always have the energy to reach across that space, even when I can tell they’re trying. And then I feel bad, because I’m probably coming off cold or distracted, and that’s not what I mean to do.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really known what to call it. I just know I don’t feel as connected as I think I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, activities, or situations because they remind you of something upsetting or stressful?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I probably admit out loud.\n\nAt work, I go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay. I’ll take a longer route, even if it doesn’t make sense, even if someone would look at it and say I’m wasting steps. I know exactly where I’m turning and why. I’ll pretend I need to check something else first, or I’ll circle around so I don’t have to go by there. It’s not like I stand there making a big decision every time — it’s automatic now. I just… don’t want to be near it.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If someone brings it up, I try to shut it down or change the subject. I’ll say I don’t remember much, or I don’t want to get into it, or I’ll suddenly need to do something. It’s not because I don’t care. It’s because once people start asking questions, I can feel myself getting pulled back into it, and I just can’t do that, especially not standing there at work like everything is normal.\n\nEven outside of work, there are things I’ve stopped doing. I used to like going to those industrial-history talks — old factories, old machinery, how things were built, that kind of thing. I know that probably sounds dry, but I enjoyed it. But after the accident, the pictures of the old machines started bothering me. They’d put up slides, big gears, belts, presses, metal parts, and I’d feel myself tense up before I could even think. So I stopped going. I told myself I was just busy or tired, but really I didn’t want to sit in a room and get hit with those images.\n\nSo yes, I avoid things. I don’t like that I do, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re more angry or irritable even in situations where there’s no real threat or reason for it, like a constant sense of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually… that’s part of why I’m here, if I’m being honest. I keep snapping at people, and sometimes it’s like I hear myself doing it a second too late.\n\nThere was a safety meeting not long ago, and someone started talking through a procedure, and I don’t even remember them saying anything that bad. It wasn’t like anyone was attacking me. But I felt this heat come up in me, just instant, and before I could slow down I was yelling. Not raising my voice a little — yelling. In front of everybody. And once I started, I couldn’t seem to rein it back in fast enough.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act different around me. They’re careful. They’ll come up like they’re afraid to ask me a question, or they’ll ask someone else instead. I can see it on their faces, and that makes me feel awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I used to be someone people could come to. I knew the job, I’d explain things, I didn’t mind helping. Now I feel like they’re all waiting to see if I’m going to go off.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. And it scares me a little, honestly, because most of the time there isn’t any real reason for it. I just feel wound tight, like everything hits me wrong, and then my mouth is already moving before I’ve had a chance to think.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the accident you mentioned earlier? Specifically, what happened, and how did you feel during and after it?\n\nPatient: I can try. It’s hard because part of what bothers me is that I don’t have it in one clean piece, like a story I can just tell from start to finish.\n\nI remember being on shift. I remember normal things before it — the floor, the noise, people moving around, the kind of routine you don’t think about because you’ve done it so many times. And I remember the first alarm starting. That part is there. I can hear it when I think about it, or I think I can. I remember that sudden feeling of, “What is that?” and everybody sort of turning their heads.\n\nBut then after that… there’s a gap. A real gap. I’ve tried to force it, and I’ve tried to go over it in my head like if I line up the pieces enough times, the middle will come back, but it doesn’t. I remember the shift, and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s this whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank. No matter how hard I try, I can’t piece it together.\n\nAnd that’s one of the worst parts, honestly, because I feel like I should know. I was there. I was right there, or close enough. People have asked me what happened, what I saw, what order things happened in, and I hate that I can’t give a proper answer. I can tell you what I remember before, and I can tell you what I remember after — the yelling, the confusion, seeing him down — but the middle is just… gone.\n\nDuring it, I don’t even know what I felt for that missing part. Afterward, I remember feeling stunned, like my body was moving but my mind hadn’t caught up. And then this horrible pressure, like I was supposed to understand what had just happened and I couldn’t. I still can’t, not all the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced flashbacks where you feel like you’re reliving the accident, like it’s happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I didn’t used to call them that, because I thought a flashback meant you completely didn’t know where you were or something, but… yes, that happens to me.\n\nIt can be something small. A cart hitting a seam in the floor, that sharp metal clank, and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or the smell of hot oil — not even strong, just that warm machinery smell — and suddenly I’m not just remembering it. I’m back in that bay. It’s like the room I’m standing in drops away for a second and I can see him there by the machine.\n\nIt feels very present. That’s the part that scares me. It’s not like thinking, “Oh, that reminds me of the accident.” It’s more like it’s in front of me again, like I’m seeing it happen all over, and I get this horrible rush through me. I can’t always tell how long it lasts. Sometimes it’s probably only a few seconds, but it doesn’t feel like that when it’s happening.\n\nAnd then after, I’m left standing there trying to act normal, trying to get my bearings, and I feel embarrassed because maybe nobody else even noticed anything happened. But inside, it feels like I’ve just been dragged right back into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting recklessly or doing things that could be dangerous, either on purpose or without thinking about the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it reckless, exactly. I mean… not in the way I think of that word. I’m not out looking for danger, if that’s what you mean. I’m not trying to hurt myself or prove something.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. There are times I do things too fast, or I’m not as careful as I used to be, but I don’t know if that’s recklessness or just being distracted or upset. I haven’t really thought of it as, “I’m doing something dangerous.” It’s more like afterward I might think, “Why did I handle that like that?” But I’m not sure that’s the same thing.\n\nSo… I don’t think so, not clearly. Nothing comes to mind where I’d say I was deliberately taking risks or doing dangerous things on purpose. If you mean something more specific, maybe ask me that way, because I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you often feel negative emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame that don’t seem to go away, even when you try to push them aside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I have feelings about it, of course, but whether they’re there all the time and don’t go away… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I think I’m angry, but then I’m not sure if it’s anger or just being worn down. And guilt or shame — I don’t know. Those words feel so big when you say them like that. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just having a bad day, or if I’m upset for a reason. Fear, maybe at times, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant or like it never leaves.\n\nI guess what I can say is I do get emotional about it, and I don’t always have as much control over that as I’d like. But I’m not sure I can honestly sort it into fear, anger, guilt, or shame and say, yes, this is always there. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a persistent sense of guilt, shame, or feeling like you are a bad person since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to what it is. Maybe not “I’m a bad person” in the simple way, like I think I’m evil or something, but I do feel… marked by it. Like something about me got ruined in a way I can’t explain.\n\nI spent so many years being good at that job. I knew what I was looking at, I knew when something sounded wrong, when something didn’t look right, how to catch things other people might miss. That was part of how I saw myself. And now some days I feel like damaged goods, honestly. Like I can’t even trust myself to do the work I built my whole life around. That makes me feel ashamed, even saying it.\n\nAnd I don’t trust that plant anymore. I don’t trust the people running it, either. I keep thinking, if things had been different, if people had cared more, if somebody had taken things seriously… I don’t know. That’s where my mind goes. So then it gets all tangled up — anger at them, shame at myself, guilt that I’m not handling it better.\n\nSo yes, there is this kind of persistent feeling that I’m not the same person, and not in a good way. Like I’m broken now, or less capable, and I hate admitting that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no… not in a clear way. I mean, I haven’t really been thinking about hobbies in a neat category like that, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had some big list of things I loved doing and now I can point to it and say, “I don’t care about any of this anymore.”\n\nI’m 59, I’ve worked a long time, and a lot of my day-to-day has always been pretty practical — work, keeping up with things, getting through the week. So when you ask about pleasure or activities, I kind of have to stop and think, “What did I used to do just for enjoyment?” And I don’t have a clean answer ready.\n\nThere are times I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest. Sometimes it feels more like I’m tired, or my mind’s somewhere else, or I just don’t have the patience for things. But I can’t honestly say, yes, I’ve lost interest in everything I used to enjoy. I haven’t really noticed it in that specific way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can say that for sure. I mean, maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really measured it that way, like “this used to feel good and now it doesn’t.” When I hear you ask it, I start trying to compare before and after, and it gets kind of slippery in my head.

There are days where things feel flat, or like I’m just going through the motions, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying things anymore. Sometimes I think I’m just tired, or distracted, or not in the right frame of mind. And then other times something ordinary can still be okay, so I hesitate to say I don’t get enjoyment out of anything.

I guess I haven’t really been looking forward to things in the way maybe you mean, but I also don’t know if that’s a change in enjoyment or just me not paying attention to that part of my life. So… I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes on that.

It’s not always that I can point to one thing and say, “That’s what I’m worried about.” It’s more like my body is already acting like something is wrong before my mind has caught up.\n\nEven in places where nothing is happening—like the break room at work—I don’t really relax. I’ll be sitting there with a cup of coffee, trying to just have a minute, and I’m still watching the door. I notice who comes in, who’s standing behind me, who’s moving too close. If there’s a sudden machine noise or something shifts in the building, I’m listening for it right away, like I have to figure out whether it means trouble. Other people just keep talking, and I’m already turned toward the sound.\n\nIt happens outside of work too, which is what makes me feel kind of… embarrassed, I guess. At rehearsal, I should be paying attention to what we’re doing, but part of me is still checking exits, watching who’s moving around behind me, listening for sudden noises. It’s like I can tell myself, “You’re fine, nothing is happening,” but my body doesn’t believe me. It stays on alert anyway.\n\nSo yes, I feel anxious even when there’s no clear reason. Not always in a panicky way, but like I can’t fully settle. Like something could go wrong at any second, and if I’m not paying attention, I’ll miss it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting startled very easily, like by sudden noises or someone coming up behind you, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I used to, definitely.\n\nIt’s like my body reacts before I even know what I’m reacting to. If I’m in the break room and there’s a sudden machine noise, or something bangs, or even somebody walks in too quietly behind me, I’ll jump or turn fast. And then I feel foolish because sometimes nobody else even reacts. They’re just sitting there drinking coffee, and I’m already looking toward the door or trying to figure out what that sound was.\n\nAt rehearsal it happens too. I’ll be trying to focus, but if someone moves behind me or a door opens suddenly, I notice it right away. I’m checking where people are, where the exits are, who’s coming in and out. It’s not that I want to be doing that. It’s just… my body won’t believe I’m safe, I guess. Even when I know nothing is happening, I’m still bracing for something.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body just refuses to power down. I’ll lie there and lie there, and the room is quiet but I’m not quiet inside. I’ll jerk awake from dreams, and in the dreams there are line alarms going off, and that awful metal sound… I can hear it so clearly that when I wake up I’m still kind of listening for it. My heart’s going and I have to figure out where I am, like I’m not fully back yet.\n\nSo even if I manage a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. It’s not just remembering it in a normal way, like thinking about something that happened. But after the accident, the pictures of the old machines started bothering me. They’d put up slides, big gears, belts, presses, metal parts, and I’d feel myself tense up before I could even think. So I stopped going. I told myself I was just busy or tired, but really I didn’t want to sit in a room and get hit with those images.\n\nSo yes, I avoid things. I don’t like that I do, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re more angry or irritable even in situations where there’s no real threat or reason for it, like a constant sense of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes. I keep snapping at people, and sometimes it’s like I hear myself doing it a second too late.\n\nThere was a safety meeting not long ago, and someone started talking through a procedure, and I don’t even remember them saying anything that bad. And once I started, I couldn’t seem to rein it back in fast enough.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act different around me. They’re careful. I remember normal things before it — the floor, the noise, people moving around, the kind of routine you don’t think about because you’ve done it so many times. I remember the shift, and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s this whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank. I can tell you what I remember before, and I can tell you what I remember after — the yelling, the confusion, seeing him down — but the middle is just… gone.\n\nDuring it, I don’t even know what I felt for that missing part. Afterward, I remember feeling stunned, like my body was moving but my mind hadn’t caught up. And then this horrible pressure, like I was supposed to understand what had just happened and I couldn’t. I still can’t, not all the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced flashbacks where you feel like you’re reliving the accident, like it’s happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I didn’t used to call them that, because I thought a flashback meant you completely didn’t know where you were or something, but… yes, that happens to me.\n\nIt can be something small. I can’t always tell how long it lasts. Sometimes it’s probably only a few seconds, but it doesn’t feel like that when it’s happening.\n\nAnd then after, I’m left standing there trying to act normal, trying to get my bearings, and I feel embarrassed because maybe nobody else even noticed anything happened. But inside, it feels like I’ve just been dragged right back into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting recklessly or doing things that could be dangerous, either on purpose or without thinking about the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it reckless, exactly. I mean… not in the way I think of that word. I’m not out looking for danger, if that’s what you mean. I’m not trying to hurt myself or prove something.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. There are times I do things too fast, or I’m not as careful as I used to be, but I don’t know if that’s recklessness or just being distracted or upset. I haven’t really thought of it as, “I’m doing something dangerous.” It’s more like afterward I might think, “Why did I handle that like that?” But I’m not sure that’s the same thing.\n\nSo… I don’t think so, not clearly. Nothing comes to mind where I’d say I was deliberately taking risks or doing dangerous things on purpose. If you mean something more specific, maybe ask me that way, because I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you often feel negative emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame that don’t seem to go away, even when you try to push them aside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I have feelings about it, of course, but whether they’re there all the time and don’t go away… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I think I’m angry, but then I’m not sure if it’s anger or just being worn down. And guilt or shame — I don’t know. Those words feel so big when you say them like that. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just having a bad day, or if I’m upset for a reason. Fear, maybe at times, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant or like it never leaves.\n\nI guess what I can say is I do get emotional about it, and I don’t always have as much control over that as I’d like. But I’m not sure I can honestly sort it into fear, anger, guilt, or shame and say, yes, this is always there. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a persistent sense of guilt, shame, or feeling like you are a bad person since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to what it is. Maybe not “I’m a bad person” in the simple way, like I think I’m evil or something, but I do feel… marked by it. Like something about me got ruined in a way I can’t explain.\n\nI spent so many years being good at that job. I knew what I was looking at, I knew when something sounded wrong, when something didn’t look right, how to catch things other people might miss. That was part of how I saw myself. And now some days I feel like damaged goods, honestly. Like I can’t even trust myself to do the work I built my whole life around. That makes me feel ashamed, even saying it.\n\nAnd I don’t trust that plant anymore. I don’t trust the people running it, either. I keep thinking, if things had been different, if people had cared more, if somebody had taken things seriously… I don’t know. That’s where my mind goes. So then it gets all tangled up — anger at them, shame at myself, guilt that I’m not handling it better.\n\nSo yes, there is this kind of persistent feeling that I’m not the same person, and not in a good way. Like I’m broken now, or less capable, and I hate admitting that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no… not in a clear way. I mean, I haven’t really been thinking about hobbies in a neat category like that, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had some big list of things I loved doing and now I can point to it and say, “I don’t care about any of this anymore.”\n\nI’m 59, I’ve worked a long time, and a lot of my day-to-day has always been pretty practical — work, keeping up with things, getting through the week. So when you ask about pleasure or activities, I kind of have to stop and think, “What did I used to do just for enjoyment?” And I don’t have a clean answer ready.\n\nThere are times I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest. Sometimes it feels more like I’m tired, or my mind’s somewhere else, or I just don’t have the patience for things. But I can’t honestly say, yes, I’ve lost interest in everything I used to enjoy. I haven’t really noticed it in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t get as much enjoyment out of things as you used to, even things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that for sure. I mean, maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really measured it that way, like “this used to feel good and now it doesn’t.” When I hear you ask it, I start trying to compare before and after, and it gets kind of slippery in my head.\n\nThere are days where things feel flat, or like I’m just going through the motions, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying things anymore. Sometimes I think I’m just tired, or distracted, or not in the right frame of mind. And then other times something ordinary can still be okay, so I hesitate to say I don’t get enjoyment out of anything.\n\nI guess I haven’t really been looking forward to things in the way maybe you mean, but I also don’t know if that’s a change in enjoyment or just me not paying attention to that part of my life. Then afterward I think, why was I so mad about that?\n\nDoctor: Do you notice any other changes in how you feel throughout the day, like feeling down, hopeless, or losing interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have noticed changes, but it’s hard to separate it all out. I don’t know if I’d call it “down” exactly every time, but there are days where I just feel… worn out in a way that’s not normal tired. Like something in me got changed and I can’t quite get back to who I was before.\n\nA big part of it is I don’t trust that plant anymore. I don’t trust the people running it, either. And that’s hard for me to say, because I’ve spent a long time doing this kind of work. I used to feel like I knew where I stood, like I understood the job and the rules and what mattered. Now I walk around with this feeling that I’m always watching for what they’re not saying, or what they’re going to let slide, and it makes everything feel poisoned, I guess.\n\nAnd then some days it turns inward. I start thinking maybe I’m the problem now. Like I’m damaged goods. I hate saying that, but that’s what it feels like sometimes. I spent my life getting good at this job, being steady, knowing what I was looking at, and now there are days where I feel like I can’t even do that right anymore. Like the thing I was proud of is the thing I’m failing at. So yes… I do feel different during the day. Heavier, I suppose. Less sure of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling anxious or worried even when there’s no clear reason for it, like a general sense that something isn’t right or that something bad might happen?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s probably the closest way to say it. It’s not always that I can point to one thing and say, “That’s what I’m worried about.” It’s more like my body is already acting like something is wrong before my mind has caught up.\n\nEven in places where nothing is happening—like the break room at work—I don’t really relax. I’ll be sitting there with a cup of coffee, trying to just have a minute, and I’m still watching the door. I notice who comes in, who’s standing behind me, who’s moving too close. If there’s a sudden machine noise or something shifts in the building, I’m listening for it right away, like I have to figure out whether it means trouble. Other people just keep talking, and I’m already turned toward the sound.\n\nIt happens outside of work too, which is what makes me feel kind of… embarrassed, I guess. At rehearsal, I should be paying attention to what we’re doing, but part of me is still checking exits, watching who’s moving around behind me, listening for sudden noises. It’s like I can tell myself, “You’re fine, nothing is happening,” but my body doesn’t believe me. It stays on alert anyway.\n\nSo yes, I feel anxious even when there’s no clear reason. Not always in a panicky way, but like I can’t fully settle. Like something could go wrong at any second, and if I’m not paying attention, I’ll miss it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself getting startled very easily, like by sudden noises or someone coming up behind you, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I used to, definitely.\n\nIt’s like my body reacts before I even know what I’m reacting to. If I’m in the break room and there’s a sudden machine noise, or something bangs, or even somebody walks in too quietly behind me, I’ll jump or turn fast. And then I feel foolish because sometimes nobody else even reacts. They’re just sitting there drinking coffee, and I’m already looking toward the door or trying to figure out what that sound was.\n\nAt rehearsal it happens too. I’ll be trying to focus, but if someone moves behind me or a door opens suddenly, I notice it right away. I’m checking where people are, where the exits are, who’s coming in and out. It’s not that I want to be doing that. It’s just… my body won’t believe I’m safe, I guess. Even when I know nothing is happening, I’m still bracing for something.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble falling asleep or staying asleep, even when you’re tired?\n\nPatient: Yes. Most nights, honestly. Even when I’m exhausted, I’ll get into bed and it’s like my body just refuses to power down. I’ll lie there and lie there, and the room is quiet but I’m not quiet inside. Sometimes it gets so late it’s almost morning before I actually fall asleep, like near dawn, and then I know I have to get up again soon, which just makes it worse.\n\nAnd when I do sleep, it doesn’t feel steady. I’ll jerk awake from dreams, and in the dreams there are line alarms going off, and that awful metal sound… I can hear it so clearly that when I wake up I’m still kind of listening for it. My heart’s going and I have to figure out where I am, like I’m not fully back yet.\n\nSo even if I manage a few hours, it doesn’t feel like real sleep. I get up feeling like I never really slept at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having intrusive thoughts or memories about a specific event that’s been bothering you, especially ones that come up even when you don’t want them to?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. It’s not just remembering it in a normal way, like thinking about something that happened. It comes in when I’m not trying to think about it at all.\n\nSometimes it’s something small that sets it off. A cart will clank at work, just that sharp metal sound, and all of a sudden I’m not really standing where I am anymore. I’m right back in that bay. Or the smell of hot oil… that one is bad. It’s like the smell gets in my throat, and then I can see my coworker by the machine again, like it’s happening right in front of me all over again.\n\nI don’t want to see it. I really don’t. I’ll be doing something ordinary, trying to keep my hands busy, trying to stay focused, and then there it is. The machine, the bay, that moment. It feels so clear, not fuzzy like an old memory should feel. It just pushes its way in, and I can’t always make it stop once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling emotionally numb or detached from the people around you, like you’re not really connecting with them even when you want to?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nI mean, I know I’ve been different with people, but I don’t know if I’d call it numb. Sometimes I feel like I’m there but I’m also watching myself be there, if that makes sense. Like somebody will be talking to me and I know I’m supposed to respond in a normal way, so I nod or say the right thing, but inside I’m kind of… blank. Or not blank exactly, just far away.\n\nBut I don’t know if that’s the same as being detached. I still care about people. I don’t want them to think I don’t. It’s more like I don’t always have the energy to reach across that space, even when I can tell they’re trying. And then I feel bad, because I’m probably coming off cold or distracted, and that’s not what I mean to do.\n\nSo… yes and no, I guess. I haven’t really known what to call it. I just know I don’t feel as connected as I think I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain places, activities, or situations because they remind you of something upsetting or stressful?\n\nPatient: Yes. I do, more than I probably admit out loud.\n\nAt work, I go out of my way not to pass the inspection bay. I’ll take a longer route, even if it doesn’t make sense, even if someone would look at it and say I’m wasting steps. I know exactly where I’m turning and why. I’ll pretend I need to check something else first, or I’ll circle around so I don’t have to go by there. It’s not like I stand there making a big decision every time — it’s automatic now. I just… don’t want to be near it.\n\nAnd I don’t talk about the accident if I can help it. If someone brings it up, I try to shut it down or change the subject. I’ll say I don’t remember much, or I don’t want to get into it, or I’ll suddenly need to do something. It’s not because I don’t care. It’s because once people start asking questions, I can feel myself getting pulled back into it, and I just can’t do that, especially not standing there at work like everything is normal.\n\nEven outside of work, there are things I’ve stopped doing. I used to like going to those industrial-history talks — old factories, old machinery, how things were built, that kind of thing. I know that probably sounds dry, but I enjoyed it. But after the accident, the pictures of the old machines started bothering me. They’d put up slides, big gears, belts, presses, metal parts, and I’d feel myself tense up before I could even think. So I stopped going. I told myself I was just busy or tired, but really I didn’t want to sit in a room and get hit with those images.\n\nSo yes, I avoid things. I don’t like that I do, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re more angry or irritable even in situations where there’s no real threat or reason for it, like a constant sense of being on edge?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually… that’s part of why I’m here, if I’m being honest. I keep snapping at people, and sometimes it’s like I hear myself doing it a second too late.\n\nThere was a safety meeting not long ago, and someone started talking through a procedure, and I don’t even remember them saying anything that bad. It wasn’t like anyone was attacking me. But I felt this heat come up in me, just instant, and before I could slow down I was yelling. Not raising my voice a little — yelling. In front of everybody. And once I started, I couldn’t seem to rein it back in fast enough.\n\nAnd now the younger inspectors… they act different around me. They’re careful. They’ll come up like they’re afraid to ask me a question, or they’ll ask someone else instead. I can see it on their faces, and that makes me feel awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I used to be someone people could come to. I knew the job, I’d explain things, I didn’t mind helping. Now I feel like they’re all waiting to see if I’m going to go off.\n\nIt’s embarrassing. And it scares me a little, honestly, because most of the time there isn’t any real reason for it. I just feel wound tight, like everything hits me wrong, and then my mouth is already moving before I’ve had a chance to think.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about the accident you mentioned earlier? Specifically, what happened, and how did you feel during and after it?\n\nPatient: I can try. It’s hard because part of what bothers me is that I don’t have it in one clean piece, like a story I can just tell from start to finish.\n\nI remember being on shift. I remember normal things before it — the floor, the noise, people moving around, the kind of routine you don’t think about because you’ve done it so many times. And I remember the first alarm starting. That part is there. I can hear it when I think about it, or I think I can. I remember that sudden feeling of, “What is that?” and everybody sort of turning their heads.\n\nBut then after that… there’s a gap. A real gap. I’ve tried to force it, and I’ve tried to go over it in my head like if I line up the pieces enough times, the middle will come back, but it doesn’t. I remember the shift, and I remember people yelling afterward, but there’s this whole stretch between the first alarm and seeing him down that is just blank. No matter how hard I try, I can’t piece it together.\n\nAnd that’s one of the worst parts, honestly, because I feel like I should know. I was there. I was right there, or close enough. People have asked me what happened, what I saw, what order things happened in, and I hate that I can’t give a proper answer. I can tell you what I remember before, and I can tell you what I remember after — the yelling, the confusion, seeing him down — but the middle is just… gone.\n\nDuring it, I don’t even know what I felt for that missing part. Afterward, I remember feeling stunned, like my body was moving but my mind hadn’t caught up. And then this horrible pressure, like I was supposed to understand what had just happened and I couldn’t. I still can’t, not all the way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced flashbacks where you feel like you’re reliving the accident, like it’s happening all over again?\n\nPatient: Yes. I didn’t used to call them that, because I thought a flashback meant you completely didn’t know where you were or something, but… yes, that happens to me.\n\nIt can be something small. A cart hitting a seam in the floor, that sharp metal clank, and my whole body reacts before I even understand why. Or the smell of hot oil — not even strong, just that warm machinery smell — and suddenly I’m not just remembering it. I’m back in that bay. It’s like the room I’m standing in drops away for a second and I can see him there by the machine.\n\nIt feels very present. That’s the part that scares me. It’s not like thinking, “Oh, that reminds me of the accident.” It’s more like it’s in front of me again, like I’m seeing it happen all over, and I get this horrible rush through me. I can’t always tell how long it lasts. Sometimes it’s probably only a few seconds, but it doesn’t feel like that when it’s happening.\n\nAnd then after, I’m left standing there trying to act normal, trying to get my bearings, and I feel embarrassed because maybe nobody else even noticed anything happened. But inside, it feels like I’ve just been dragged right back into it.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself acting recklessly or doing things that could be dangerous, either on purpose or without thinking about the consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it reckless, exactly. I mean… not in the way I think of that word. I’m not out looking for danger, if that’s what you mean. I’m not trying to hurt myself or prove something.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. There are times I do things too fast, or I’m not as careful as I used to be, but I don’t know if that’s recklessness or just being distracted or upset. I haven’t really thought of it as, “I’m doing something dangerous.” It’s more like afterward I might think, “Why did I handle that like that?” But I’m not sure that’s the same thing.\n\nSo… I don’t think so, not clearly. Nothing comes to mind where I’d say I was deliberately taking risks or doing dangerous things on purpose. If you mean something more specific, maybe ask me that way, because I’m not sure I’m answering what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you often feel negative emotions like fear, anger, guilt, or shame that don’t seem to go away, even when you try to push them aside?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I have feelings about it, of course, but whether they’re there all the time and don’t go away… I don’t know. I haven’t really kept track of it that way.\n\nSometimes I think I’m angry, but then I’m not sure if it’s anger or just being worn down. And guilt or shame — I don’t know. Those words feel so big when you say them like that. I don’t want to say yes if I’m just having a bad day, or if I’m upset for a reason. Fear, maybe at times, but I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant or like it never leaves.\n\nI guess what I can say is I do get emotional about it, and I don’t always have as much control over that as I’d like. But I’m not sure I can honestly sort it into fear, anger, guilt, or shame and say, yes, this is always there. It’s more muddled than that for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling a persistent sense of guilt, shame, or feeling like you are a bad person since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s closer to what it is. Maybe not “I’m a bad person” in the simple way, like I think I’m evil or something, but I do feel… marked by it. Like something about me got ruined in a way I can’t explain.\n\nI spent so many years being good at that job. I knew what I was looking at, I knew when something sounded wrong, when something didn’t look right, how to catch things other people might miss. That was part of how I saw myself. And now some days I feel like damaged goods, honestly. Like I can’t even trust myself to do the work I built my whole life around. That makes me feel ashamed, even saying it.\n\nAnd I don’t trust that plant anymore. I don’t trust the people running it, either. I keep thinking, if things had been different, if people had cared more, if somebody had taken things seriously… I don’t know. That’s where my mind goes. So then it gets all tangled up — anger at them, shame at myself, guilt that I’m not handling it better.\n\nSo yes, there is this kind of persistent feeling that I’m not the same person, and not in a good way. Like I’m broken now, or less capable, and I hate admitting that.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in things you used to enjoy, like hobbies or activities that used to bring you pleasure?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no… not in a clear way. I mean, I haven’t really been thinking about hobbies in a neat category like that, if that makes sense. It’s not like I had some big list of things I loved doing and now I can point to it and say, “I don’t care about any of this anymore.”\n\nI’m 59, I’ve worked a long time, and a lot of my day-to-day has always been pretty practical — work, keeping up with things, getting through the week. So when you ask about pleasure or activities, I kind of have to stop and think, “What did I used to do just for enjoyment?” And I don’t have a clean answer ready.\n\nThere are times I don’t feel like doing much, but I don’t know if that’s the same as losing interest. Sometimes it feels more like I’m tired, or my mind’s somewhere else, or I just don’t have the patience for things. But I can’t honestly say, yes, I’ve lost interest in everything I used to enjoy. I haven’t really noticed it in that specific way.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you don’t get as much enjoyment out of things as you used to, even things you used to look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can say that for sure. I mean, maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really measured it that way, like “this used to feel good and now it doesn’t.” When I hear you ask it, I start trying to compare before and after, and it gets kind of slippery in my head.\n\nThere are days where things feel flat, or like I’m just going through the motions, but I don’t know if that’s the same as not enjoying things anymore. Sometimes I think I’m just tired, or distracted, or not in the right frame of mind. And then other times something ordinary can still be okay, so I hesitate to say I don’t get enjoyment out of anything.\n\nI guess I haven’t really been looking forward to things in the way maybe you mean, but I also don’t know if that’s a change in enjoyment or just me not paying attention to that part of my life. So… I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes on that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I don’t know that I’ve been paying close enough attention to “positive emotions” in that way, like measuring whether happiness or love is weaker than it used to be.

I mean… I still know when something matters to me. I still know who I care about. It’s not like I feel nothing, or like I’m empty all the time. But whether it feels as strong as before? I don’t know. That’s hard for me to separate out. Sometimes I’m just tired or preoccupied, and then I might seem kind of flat, but I don’t know if that means I can’t feel good emotions or if I’m just not in the moment.

So I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is. If you mean, “Do I never feel happy or caring?” no, that’s not right. If you mean, “Does it all feel a little harder to reach sometimes?” maybe, but I’m not sure.

I knew what I was looking at, I knew when something sounded wrong, when something didn’t look right, how to catch things other people might miss. That was part of how I saw myself. Sometimes I think I’m just tired, or distracted, or not in the right frame of mind. And then other times something ordinary can still be okay, so I hesitate to say I don’t get enjoyment out of anything.\n\nI guess I haven’t really been looking forward to things in the way maybe you mean, but I also don’t know if that’s a change in enjoyment or just me not paying attention to that part of my life. So… I’m not sure. I can’t give you a clean yes on that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not feeling good emotions, like happiness or love, as strongly as you used to, even during moments that should feel positive or meaningful?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that one cleanly. I still know who I care about. It’s not like I feel nothing, or like I’m empty all the time. But whether it feels as strong as before? I don’t know. That’s hard for me to separate out. Sometimes I’m just tired or preoccupied, and then I might seem kind of flat, but I don’t know if that means I can’t feel good emotions or if I’m just not in the moment.\n\nSo I guess I’d say I haven’t really noticed that specifically. I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound more definite than it is. If you mean, “Do I never feel happy or caring?” no, that’s not right. If you mean, “Does it all feel a little harder to reach sometimes?” maybe, but I’m not sure."
